<summary>This is the title page for an 1803 publication that claims to expose the secret seven-grade system of mystical Freemasonry and the Order of the Knights of Light. It was published posthumously from the papers of a deceased member to inform both initiates and the general public.</summary>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface to a work detailing the "Clerics of the Temple," a high-degree branch of Freemasonry. It introduces Johann August Starck and the nature of his controversial ritual system.</meta>

# Preface

---

**T**he system of the Clerics of the Templars <note>The "Cleriker der Tempelherrn" was a branch of Freemasonry founded by Johann August Starck, which claimed that the secret knowledge of the Knights Templar was preserved by an inner circle of priests (clerics) rather than just knights.</note>, which the Chief Court Chaplain Starck in Darmstadt—known by the order-name Brother Archidemides—so vigorously defended and sought to spread with all his might, appears here printed truly according to the official records. This system is of interest not only to the Masons <note>original: "Maurer"</note> but also especially to the scholarly world. All the Catholic customs and Catholic ceremonies used in divine service—which have, of course, been corrupted by the passage of time, and in which the ancient Greek ritual and the ancient Greek 

<summary>This preface introduces a documentary account of Johann August Starck’s "Clerics of the Temple," a Masonic system claiming to preserve ancient ecclesiastical and Greek rituals.</summary>
<keywords>Johann August Starck, Clerics of the Templars, Freemasonry, Darmstadt, religious ritual, Archidemides</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Clerical system of Freemasonry, shifting from a description of the ritual's purported ancient origins to the author's stated purpose: exposing the system as a deceptive scheme.</meta>

liturgy still possess the most truth; all these customs, I say, according to this system, possess wisdom in reserve and, as far as their meaning is concerned, teem with the deepest secrets. — One should therefore not only read through this writing, but also study it. —

It is truly high time at last to lay open before the eyes of the world the web of malice and deceit through which people are led astray, deprived of their money, and even shackled in their reason and understanding, and to bring a welcome end to all these swindles and frauds. This little book fulfills this purpose and is therefore rightfully recommended to both Masons <note>original: "Maurern"</note> and non-Masons <note>The author frames the book as an "exposé," a popular 18th-century genre where former members or critics revealed the "secrets" of societies like the Freemasons or Illuminati to the public.</note>. The second part will appear immediately after the Easter Fair <note>original: "Ostermesse"; while "Messe" can mean a religious Mass, in the context of the German book trade, it refers to the major commercial book fairs held in Leipzig and Frankfurt.</note>.

***

<summary>The author concludes the preface by claiming that while these Masonic rituals appear to hold deep ancient wisdom, they are actually part of a deceptive scheme to defraud the public. The book is presented as a necessary exposure of these "swindles" to liberate the reader's reason.</summary>
<keywords>liturgy, secrecy, Masonic exposé, fraud, religious deception, Easter Fair, Enlightenment</keywords>

# Apprentice Degree <note>original: "Lehrlings-Grad." The Apprentice is the first level of initiation in Freemasonry. In this stage, the initiate is likened to a "rough ashlar"—an unfinished stone—that must be shaped through moral work and discipline.</note>

->Sirach Chapter 22, verse 33. <note>original: "Jes. Syr." refers to Jesus Sirach, also known as Ecclesiasticus or the Wisdom of Sirach. This biblical quote highlights the virtue of silence, which is the primary obligation of the Apprentice Mason.</note><-

# Opening of the Apprentice Lodge.

->Venerable Master: — — o<-
->1st Warden: — — o      2nd Warden: o<-

## Prayer.

---

Brother 1st Warden! What are the duties of the Brethren before the opening of the Apprentice <term>Lodge<gloss>The original text uses the square symbol ▢ to denote the Lodge</gloss></term>?
To see whether the Lodge is <term>tyled<gloss>original: "gedeckt" — meaning secured or guarded by a "Tyler" to ensure privacy from the uninitiated</gloss></term> and whether no <term>profane person<gloss>original: "Unheiliger" — literally "unholy one," referring to those not initiated into the order</gloss></term> desecrates our temple.
Brother Wardens: fulfill these duties.
The Wardens have the Lodge tyled, and place the last-admitted member, sword in hand, at the inner door of the sanctuary. Every Brother then allows himself to be given the word, the touch, and the <term>kiss of peace<gloss>a ritual gesture of fellowship and trust among members</gloss></term> by every Brother on his <term>column<gloss>original: "Colonne" — referring to the rows of seating on the north and south sides of the Lodge room</gloss></term>, and gives the <term>password<gloss>original: "Passir. B." — likely an abbreviation for Passier-Wort (password or word of passage)</gloss></term> in return. After this has occurred, both Wardens meet:

<summary>This page details the ritual security protocols for opening a Masonic Lodge, including "tyling" the door and the ceremonial exchange of signs and passwords among the members. It emphasizes the transition from the outside world into the sacred space of the "temple."</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Apprentice Lodge, Masonic ritual, tyling, Wardens, Venerable Master, kiss of peace, sanctuary, initiation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the opening ritual of the Apprentice Lodge. The previous page concluded with the Wardens performing a ceremonial exchange of the password. This page focuses on the formal catechism—the question-and-answer session—between the Venerable Master and the Wardens to establish their roles and the purpose of the meeting.</meta>

...together before the altar, and the 2nd gives the word to the 1st, which the latter must give to the <term>Venerable Master<gloss>original: "Ew. M." — abbreviation for Ehrwürdiger Meister, the presiding officer of the Lodge</gloss></term>, whereupon both return to their places.

**Venerable Master:** Brother 1st Warden! Where is the Venerable Master?
**1st Warden:** In the East.
**Venerable Master:** Why?
**1st Warden:** Because the sun begins its course in this region of the world, and just as the sun illuminates the world, the Venerable Master illuminates our labors.
**Venerable Master:** Brother 1st Warden, are you an <term>Entered Apprentice<gloss>original: "M. L." — abbreviation for Maurer-Lehrling (Masonic Apprentice), the first degree of Freemasonry</gloss></term>?
**1st Warden:** My <term>Brothers<gloss>original: "B. B." — abbreviation for Brüder (plural)</gloss></term> recognize me as such.
**Venerable Master:** By what do I know that you are one?
**1st Warden:** By my signs, words, and the circumstances of my initiation.
**Venerable Master:** Give me the sign! <note>The symbol $\Delta$ indicates the performance of a specific ritual gesture associated with the degree</note>
**Venerable Master:** Brother 2nd Warden, why have you dedicated yourself to Masonry?
**2nd Warden:** Because I was in darkness and requested enlightenment.
**Venerable Master:** By what means were you introduced into the <term>Lodge<gloss>represented by the symbol ▢</gloss></term>?
**2nd Warden:** By three strong knocks.
**Venerable Master:** What do these signify?
**2nd Warden:** The words of <term>Holy Scripture<gloss>original: "h. S." — abbreviation for heilige Schrift</gloss></term>: "Ask, and it shall be given to you; seek, and you shall find; knock, and it shall be opened unto you." <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 7:7</note>
**Venerable Master:** My Brothers, let us make use of this precept; let us work tirelessly to obtain more light, so that we...

...may we approach **Masonic perfection** ever more.
**Brother 1st <term>Warden<gloss>original: "B." — abbreviation for Aufseher (Warden)</gloss></term>:** tell me the <term>Apprentice Word<gloss>original: "L.: W.:" — abbreviation for Lehrlings-Wort</gloss></term>.
Tell me the 1st letter, I will tell you the 2nd. J — n. <note>The letters J—n refer to Jachin, the name of the right-hand pillar of Solomon's Temple, used as a recognition word for the first degree.</note>
**Brother 2nd Warden:** give me the **Pass-word**?
T — n. <note>T—n refers to Tubal-cain, a name from Genesis traditionally used as a password in various Masonic degrees.</note>
**Brother 1st Warden:** when do the <term>Masonic Lodges<gloss>original: "M.: L.:"</gloss></term> begin their <term>Labors<gloss>original: "A.:" — abbreviation for Arbeiten</gloss></term>?
At Noon.
What time is it?
Full Noon.
Since it is Noon, announce to your <term>Columns<gloss>original: "Colonnen" — the two rows of members seated in the north and south of the lodge</gloss></term> that I will open the Apprentice <term>Lodge<gloss>represented by the symbol ☐</gloss></term> with the usual solemnities. △ <note>The triangle symbol indicates a ritual sign or a pause.</note>

My Brothers, inform your Columns that the Lodge is now open, and that the Brothers are to conduct themselves according to the respect owed to our <term>Laws<gloss>original: "G.:" — abbreviation for Gesetze</gloss></term>. △ **Brother Secretary:** read the **minutes** of the last assembly. △

## Initiation.

---

My Brothers: do you confirm the word you gave regarding the initiation of the proposed <term>Profane<gloss>original: "Unheiligen" — literally "the unholy," a standard Masonic term for a non-initiate or candidate</gloss></term>?

<meta>This page continues the initiation ritual for the Apprentice degree. Has none of you discovered anything in the candidate that would be contrary to the customs and statutes of <term>Masonry<gloss>original: "M.:"</gloss></term>? Remember that at this moment, you must set aside all respect for persons. Friendship, even ties of blood, must not make you blind to the faults of the uninitiated. Consider that our peace, our happiness, and the honor of the <term>Royal Art<gloss>original: "K.: K.:" — short for Königliche Kunst, a traditional name for Freemasonry</gloss></term> are at stake.

The votes are collected; the proposing <term>Brother<gloss>original: "B." — abbreviation for Bruder</gloss></term> <term>covers the Lodge<gloss>original: "deckt... die ☐" — This means the brother temporarily leaves the room to perform a duty outside or to ensure the door is guarded</gloss></term> during this process; should some <term>Brothers<gloss>original: "B. B." — abbreviation for Brüder</gloss></term> be opposed to the initiation, then Article 46 of the statutes is to be followed.

Brother Orator, proceed to the candidate; use all your powers to provide him with a correct concept of Masonry, so that you may see whether his dispositions make him worthy of initiation among us; see if you can discover whether it is not a wicked curiosity that leads him to explore our secrets, only to communicate them to others hereafter. △ <note>The triangle symbol indicates a ritual pause or a specific sign to be made by the officers.</note>

While the Orator covers the <term>Lodge<gloss>represented by the symbol ☐</gloss></term>, the <term>Venerable Master<gloss>original: "E.: M.:" — abbreviation for Ehrwürdiger Meister</gloss></term> reminds the Brothers of their duties in a short speech according to the enclosed schema under number 1. Whereupon the Brother Orator re-enters and gives an account of his mission.

When you, <term>Dread Brother<gloss>original: "Fürcht.: B.:" — Likely the 'Fürchterlicher Bruder' (Dread or Terrible Brother), an officer responsible for the candidate’s preparation and symbolic "terrors" during the ritual</gloss></term>, have everything ready for the initiation of a <term>profane person<gloss>original: "Unheiligen" — literally "unholy one," a standard Masonic term for a non-member</gloss></term>, go to him and instill in him a reverent—

...full of awe, and instilling a reverence toward the <term>Mysteries<gloss>original: "G.:." — likely Geheimnisse (secrets/mysteries) or Gott (God)</gloss></term> that will be communicated to him; deprive him of all metals <note>In Masonic ritual, "metals" represent worldly wealth, status, and weapons; the candidate must enter "poor and penniless" to show that internal character matters more than external riches.</note>, and dress him in the customary manner.

<term>Brother Master of Ceremonies<gloss>original: "B.:. C.:. M.:."</gloss></term>! assist the <term>Dreadful Brother<gloss>original: "fürchterlichen B.:." — traditionally referring to the Tyler or the Preparer, who adopts a stern persona to test the candidate's resolve</gloss></term>, and notify us of the arrival of the <term>Uninitiated<gloss>original: "Unheiligen" — literally "the unholy one," referring to the profane candidate who has not yet been "sanctified" by initiation</gloss></term>. △ <note>The triangle symbol indicates a ritual pause or a specific sign to be made by the officers.</note> I do not believe it necessary, my Brothers, to recommend the most solemn silence during the <term>reception<gloss>initiation ceremony</gloss></term>! <term>Brothers<gloss>original: "B.:. B.:. B.:." — plural abbreviation for Brüder</gloss></term>! You shall be unceasingly attentive to the slightest movement of the candidate, lest curiosity or terror drive him to tear the <term>blindfold<gloss>original: "Binde von den Augen"</gloss></term> from his eyes. I rely entirely upon your prudence in this matter.

The <term>Venerable Master<gloss>original: "E.:. M.:." — abbreviation for Ehrwürdiger Meister</gloss></term> enters and announces the arrival of the candidate: the Brothers proceed to their places and observe the deepest silence.

The <term>Dreadful Brother<gloss>the officer guarding the door</gloss></term> who introduces the candidate knocks ○○ — ○. <note>This represents a specific rhythmic sequence of knocks used to identify one's rank or purpose at the door.</note> The Brother on watch reports this to the <term>Second Brother<gloss>the Second Warden</gloss></term> by tapping ·· — · upon his shoulder; the Second Brother reports to the First, though in a low voice, and the latter reports to the <term>Orient<gloss>the "East," the ceremonial seat of the Master of the Lodge</gloss></term>.

According to the regulation, my Brothers. △

<term>Venerable Master<gloss>original: "V.:." — likely abbreviation for Verehrungswürdiger</gloss></term>: <term>First Brother<gloss>the First Warden</gloss></term>, see who has knocked!

<meta>This page continues the initiation ritual for the Apprentice degree. The previous page ended with the Worshipful Master’s instructions for the officers to prepare the candidate and maintain solemn silence. This page details the formal "interrogation at the door," where the candidate is questioned about his identity and motives before being physically admitted into the Lodge room.</meta>

The door is opened, and the <term>Dreadful Brother<gloss>original: "fürchterliche B∴"</gloss></term> <note>The "Dreadful Brother" is a ritual role, often the Tyler or the Preparer, tasked with intimidating the candidate to test his resolve.</note> says: "There is an uninitiated person here who demands initiation." The door is closed with violence, and the <term>Brother on Guard<gloss>original: "wachthabende B∴"</gloss></term> repeats the words of the Dreadful One. Whereupon the <term>Worshipful Master<gloss>original: "Erw∴ M∴"</gloss></term> says in a loud voice:

"Ask him for his last name, his first name, his religion, his age, and his homeland. — Ask him whether criminal curiosity or self-interest guides him, whether he takes this step of his own free impulse and his own choice, whether he is minded to submit himself to the trials he must endure before he can be admitted to our <term>Mysteries<gloss>original: "G∴" — abbreviation for Geheimnisse</gloss></term>, and who it is that proposed him."

All these questions take place one after another; at every answer, the door is closed with violence. As soon as the <term>Brother Sponsor<gloss>original: "B∴ Pate"</gloss></term> hears himself named, he steps between the two <term>Brothers<gloss>abbreviated "B∴"</gloss></term> <note>Likely referring to the Senior and Junior Wardens who stand near the Master.</note>, and the <term>Worshipful Master<gloss>original: "E∴ M∴"</gloss></term> says: "Assure us as a <term>Mason<gloss>original: "M∴"</gloss></term> and upon your word of honor that the candidate possesses all the qualities belonging to a Mason." △ <note>The triangle symbol indicates a ritual pause or a specific sign to be made by the officers.</note>

<term>My Brothers<gloss>original: "M∴ B∴ B∴" — plural abbreviation for "Meine Brüder"</gloss></term>, confirm your consent!

Hands are raised <note>This signifies a vote of approval by the members of the Lodge.</note>, and then it is said: "Long may he live! Let the candidate enter."

The Brother on Guard opens the door and says with violence: "Enter!" The Dreadful Brother pushes him inside and says: "Go under the protection" <note>The sentence ends mid-phrase as the text continues onto the next page.</note>

Your friend, who has just left us,

<meta>This page continues the Master's address to the candidate during the initiation ritual. The sentence fragment from the previous page concludes here, establishing the moral duties of a Mason toward God, country, and society, before challenging the candidate to undergo the "trials" or "journeys" of the ritual.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>— 11 —</header>

...to God, his fatherland, and his friends; all vices are an abomination to him. Our <term>statutes<gloss>German: "Satzungen"</gloss></term> aim not only at the improvement of morals, but also at the refinement of the heart and the spirit. Rashness, inconstancy, adultery, the spirit of discord, and all excesses are banished from among true <term>Brothers<gloss>German: "B.:. B.:." (Brüdern)</gloss></term>; should you have found people who do not resemble the image I have just presented to you, and who nevertheless called themselves <term>Masons<gloss>German: "M.:." (Maurer)</gloss></term>, then believe for certain that swindlers have taken advantage of your ignorance; or that they were hypocrites who deceived us, but were punished for it with shame and contempt, and no longer have any fellowship with us. We hope that we will never have cause to regret your admission to our <term>secret rites<gloss>German: "G.:. G.:." (likely Geheimen Gebräuchen)</gloss></term>, and that you will make the best use of them. I must still tell you, however, that we will put your constancy and your courage to terrible tests, which you will find impossible to pass if you are not inspired by true zeal. Do you wish, sir, to submit yourself to these trials?

Brother Second <term>Warden<gloss>German: "B.:. 2ter B.:." The oath which you are about to take must be voluntary and unconstrained; it is therefore still in your power to withdraw; however, in order to remove any doubts on your part, I must once more assure you here that it contains nothing contrary to your religious worship, your honor, or the laws. ...n; these white gloves, which you must wear in the <term>▢<gloss>German: "Loge" (Lodge)</gloss></term>, are a symbol of innocence; the <term>M.:<gloss>German: "Maurer" (Mason)</gloss></term> must never stain his hands through injustice or through the blood of his <term>B.: B.:<gloss>German: "Brüder" (Brothers)</gloss></term>; quarreling and hatred are strictly forbidden by our statutes.

These women’s gloves are intended to dispel the prejudice that we hate women; it is not from a lack of respect that we deny the other sex entry into our temples, but solely to prevent jealousy, which is entirely contrary to brotherly unity; you shall give these gloves to a person who deserves respect in the fullest sense of the word; any other use is contrary to the respect you owe to yourself and to virtue. <note>In 18th and 19th-century Masonic practice, a newly initiated Mason was often given two pairs of white gloves: one for himself and one to be given to the woman he most esteemed, usually his wife.</note>

...then you shall answer: I can only spell it; tell me the first letter, and I will tell you the second. <note>This refers to the "halving" or spelling of the sacred word, a traditional Masonic practice where the secret word is never spoken in full, but exchanged letter-by-letter between two brothers to prove their identity.</note>

B. d. W.:<gloss>Grand Architect of the Universe (German: "Großer Baumeister der Welt").</gloss></term> according to divine instructions. Not all men without distinction were admitted to the work on the same; they first had to submit to a strict examination of their morals. These chosen men received different names according to the variety of their tasks; they changed these names at different times, as circumstances required, until—

<meta>This page continues the instructional charge following a Masonic initiation. ...they received the name Builders, a name which encompasses all types of labor. In more recent times, they adopted the name Free-Masons. The customs of these masons have come down to us from age to age; they are so distinguished by the requirement of good morals and the strictest morality that they have earned the respect and attention of the greatest monarchs. <note>In the 18th century, Freemasonry often sought and received patronage from royalty, which helped establish its social legitimacy.</note> The greatest concern of the <term>M∴<gloss>Masons (German: "Maurer")</gloss></term> has always been the preservation of the examples of the purest virtues and the <term>S∴<gloss>Secrets (German: "Geheimnisse")</gloss></term> of the <term>R∴ A∴<gloss>Royal Art (German: "Königliche Kunst")</gloss></term>. <note>The "Royal Art" is a traditional Masonic term for the craft of Masonry itself, suggesting it is the highest of all human pursuits.</note> Within our symbols lie secrets, to whose disclosure you can only arrive through zeal, reflection, and hearty devotion to the statutes of the <term>R∴ A∴<gloss>Royal Art</gloss></term>. All customs, even the ceremonies of your initiation, are symbols that contain more within them than you can imagine. Work, therefore, my Brother! to reach the pinnacle of virtue and bliss that crown our building.

->My <term>D∴ B∴ B∴<gloss>Dear Brethren (German: "Die lieben Brüder")</gloss></term>, rejoice with me over the increase our society has received through such a worthy <term>B∴<gloss>Brother (German: "Bruder")</gloss></term>. Δ<- <note>The triangle symbol (Δ) often indicates a ritual pause, a sign given by the Master, or the conclusion of a formal speech in Masonic manuscripts.</note>

M.<gloss>Worshipful Master (German: "Ehrwürdiger Meister")</gloss></term>), and listen attentively to the speech that the <term>B∴ O∴<gloss>Brother Orator (German: "Bruder Redner")</gloss></term> will read to you. <note>The Orator is the Lodge officer responsible for delivering the formal "Charge" or educational lecture to the newly initiated candidate.</note> In it, he will reveal to you the <term>S∴<gloss>Secrets</gloss></term> of the <term>M∴<gloss>Masons</gloss></term> as far as the degree you have just entered allows, and will set before you the image of a true <term>M∴<gloss>Mason</gloss></term>, according to which you must model yourself in the future.

<meta>This page transitions from the instructional lecture given to the newly initiated candidate to a formal ritual exchange known as a catechism. It concludes with the protocol for closing the Lodge meeting, including the collection of charity.</meta>

### Questions upon Entering the <term>▭<gloss>Lodge (German: "Loge")</gloss></term>.

---

Where do you come from?
From the darkness. <note>In Masonic ritual, "darkness" symbolizes the state of the candidate before receiving the intellectual and spiritual "light" of the order's teachings.</note>
What are you seeking here?
The light.
Where have you been?
In the St. John’s <term>▭<gloss>Lodge</gloss></term>. <note>St. John's Lodges are the fundamental units of "Blue Lodge" Freemasonry, named after St. John the Baptist and St. John the Evangelist.</note>
What do you bring us?
Joy, health, happiness, and a good reception for the <term>W∴ M∴<gloss>Worshipful Master (German: "Ehrwürdiger Meister")</gloss></term> and all worthy <term>B∴ B∴<gloss>Brethren (German: "Brüder")</gloss></term>.
What do you want here?
To learn to overcome my passions, and to subject my will to the <term>M∴<gloss>Masonic (German: "Maurerischen")</gloss></term> laws.
What is the foremost duty of a <term>M∴<gloss>Mason</gloss></term>?
To work united with his brothers for the progress of the <term>R∴ A∴<gloss>Royal Art (German: "Königliche Kunst")</gloss></term>, in order to one day attain higher degrees. <note>The "Royal Art" is a traditional term for the practice of Freemasonry itself.</note>

---

<term>B∴<gloss>Brother</gloss></term> Treasurer, let the <term>alms box<gloss>Armenbüchse</gloss></term> be passed around. △ <note>The triangle symbol indicates a specific ritual sign or sequence of claps performed by the members.</note>
My <term>B∴ B∴<gloss>Brethren</gloss></term>, does anyone among you have anything to propose for the welfare of the <term>R∴ A∴<gloss>Royal Art</gloss></term> or for the particular benefit of this <term>▭<gloss>Lodge</gloss></term>?

## Outline No. 1 for Page 6.

My <term>B∴ B∴<gloss>Brethren</gloss></term>, we are about to open the gates to happiness for an uninitiated man; <note>The German "Ungeweihten" refers to a candidate who has not yet received Masonic light.</note> he imagines our assembly as a society of wise and virtuous men; he hopes to find here peace of soul and purity of morals. Do we wish to play the hypocrite? Do we wish to deceive him? Shall he find here all the vices that poison the society of the <term>unholy<gloss>profane</gloss></term>? <note>In Masonic tradition, "the profane" or "unholy" refers simply to those outside the order, though here the author uses it to contrast worldly vice with Masonic virtue.</note> Shall he have the right to label us with the most shameful of names, with the name "deceivers"? And we would deserve this if we promised him the true riches of the soul, and he found among us nothing but laws without power, and labors without purpose or effect; if we called ourselves wise and were in no way better than the class of people whom we label with the offensive name "profane." How could we dare to preach peace to him if we annoyed him at once with our discord? How could we boast of Masonic wisdom to him while surrendering ourselves to every passion?

O, my <term>B∴ B∴<gloss>Brethren</gloss></term>! Let us prove to him that a <term>M∴<gloss>Mason</gloss></term> is truly a wise and enlightened person—a person who approaches the great and sublime purpose of his destiny. Only insofar as we compel his respect through our actions do we have a right to his...

The Worshipful Master opens the Lodge <term>Lodge: represented in the original manuscript by a square symbol (□)</term> with a prayer to the Great Architect of the Universe, during which the Honorable Brethren remain in the posture of Masonic Apprentices.

### At Noon.

Brother ∴ 2nd Warden ∴ what is the hour? <note>original: "was ist die Glocke?"; literally "what is the bell?", a ritual inquiry into the symbolic time.</note>

It is high noon.

Brethren ∴ Wardens ∴ inform your columns <term>Columns: the two rows of seats along the North and South walls of the lodge where the members sit</term> that, because it is high noon, I shall begin the Fellow Craft work <term>Gesellenarbeit: the ritual work belonging to the second degree of Freemasonry</term> with the usual ceremonies.

M. ∴: "Ehrwürdiger Meister," the presiding officer of the lodge</term> to the East <term>Orient: the "East," the symbolic place of light where the Master sits</term>, whereupon he knocks o o — o — o — o and the Brethren do the same; the sign and the expression of approbation <note>original: "Beyfallsbezeugung"; a ritualized form of Masonic clapping used to show collective agreement or honor</note> are made, and the Brethren ∴ take their seats.

## I n i t i a t i o n .

---

My Brethren ∴: I have opened the Fellow Craft <image-desc>square symbol representing a Masonic Lodge</image-desc> in order to present to you once again the petition of Brother N. N.; he requests from you the favor of being admitted as a Fellow Craft; his masters <note>likely referring to the Master Masons who have supervised his progress as an Apprentice</note> are in his favor; do you confirm once more your previously given consent? Should anyone have learned anything regarding the candidate that might cause an obstacle to his initiation, we shall collect the votes anew.

The Brethren ∴ raise their hands as a sign of their assent; if there are any who do not do so, the votes are collected, and one proceeds as the 46th Article of the Statutes demands.

The Master of Ceremonies, who is to lead the candidate in, knocks as an Apprentice <note>A specific series of knocks identifying the person at the door as someone holding the first degree of Masonry</note>, and the Worshipful Master is informed of it in the usual manner.

It is Brother Master of Ceremonies with the candidate.

Continue to fulfill your duties as an upright man and as a good Mason, remain zealous, and you shall receive more light and a closer explanation of the purpose of our labors.

> I, N. N. <note>A placeholder for the candidate's name (Nomen Nescio)</note>, promise in the presence of the **Great Architect of the Universe** <note>original: "G. I vow never to reveal the secrets of the Masons, nor ever to entrust the secrets of the Fellowcrafts to an Apprentice. I reaffirm, taken as a whole, everything that I vowed and voluntarily swore in my obligation as an Apprentice; I also subject myself anew to all the penalties contained in the aforementioned oath, and to the dishonor, should I ever violate this obligation. So help me God.

## Instruction.

---

Are you a **Masonic Fellowcraft**?
Yes, I am.
By what shall I recognize that you are one?
By my signs, words, tokens <note>original: "Berührungen" (touches)</note>, and by the circumstances of my admission.
Will you give me the sign? △ <note>This symbol indicates a specific ritual gesture performed by the candidate</note>
What does this sign signify?
It refers to the obligation that I would rather have my heart torn from my body than reveal the secrets of **Free Masonry**.
Tell me the **Fellowcraft word**.
Give me the first letter, and I will give you the second. B — <note>original: "B" refers to Boaz, one of the two pillars of Solomon's Temple. It is common in Masonic ritual to "letter" or "halve" sacred words rather than speak them in full.</note>
What does this word mean?
That power is in God.
What did you notice upon entering the **Lodge**? <note>The symbol "☐" stands for the Lodge</note>
A great light, in the center of which was a G. <note>In the Fellowcraft degree, the letter G typically represents Geometry or God</note>
Where did this light come from?
From the **Blazing Star**. <note>The "Flammender Stern" is a central symbol of the second degree, representing the light of divine providence or human reason guiding the initiate.</note>
Tell me the ultimate purpose of your entrance.
Tell me the first, and I will give you the second.
I preserve. <note>original: "Ich verwahre." This likely refers to the "I hide/I preserve" dialogue regarding the secrets of the craft.</note>

<summary>This page contains the ritual catechism for the Fellowcraft degree, where the candidate demonstrates his knowledge of the degree's secret sign, the word Boaz, and the symbolism of the Blazing Star and the letter G.</summary>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the Masonic catechism for the Fellowcraft (second degree). The previous page ended with the candidate stating "I preserve" (or "I hide"), leading into this dialogue about the nature of Masonic secrets and the symbolic dimensions of the Lodge.</meta>

I conceal. <note>original: "Ich verberge." In Masonic ritual, this is the traditional response to "What do you do with the secrets?"</note>
What do you conceal?
The secret of **Masonry** <note>original: "M. ⸫" (Maurerei)</note> and of **Freemasonry** <note>original: "F. My. ⸫" (Freimaurerei)</note>.
Where do you conceal it?
In the heart.
Why did you seek to be received as a **Fellowcraft** <term>Geselle: The second level of Masonic initiation, corresponding to a "journeyman" who has passed beyond the stage of an apprentice.</term>?
In order to learn the meaning of the letter **G**.
What does it mean?
The **fifth science** <note>In the medieval Seven Liberal Arts, the fifth science is Geometry, which is considered the foundation of Masonry.</note>.
Does it mean nothing else?
It is the initial letter of the English word **God**, which signifies the name of the **Eternal**.
How were you received as a **Fellowcraft**?
By the passage from **Pillar J** to **Pillar B** <note>Refers to Jachin and Boaz, the two bronze pillars at the porch of Solomon's Temple. The candidate symbolically moves between them to represent progress in knowledge.</note>.
In what kind of **Lodge** <note>The symbol "▭" or "☐" represents the Lodge.</note> were you received?
In one that is just and perfect.
What is the form of your **Lodge**?
That of an **oblong square** <note>Historically, this refers to a rectangle, representing the world as it was understood in antiquity.</note>.
How is it situated?
Between **Morning** and **Evening** <note>East and West.</note>.
What is its length?
It extends from the **rising** to the **setting** <note>of the sun</note>.
How wide is it?
From **noon** to **midnight** <note>South to North.</note>.
How high?
**Infinite**.

<summary>This page contains a Masonic catechism explaining the symbolism of the letter G (representing both Geometry and God) and the ritual passage between the pillars. It also describes the Lodge as a universal space, with dimensions spanning the entire world and reaching into the infinite.</summary>
<keywords>Fellowcraft degree, Letter G, Geometry, God, Jachin and Boaz, Oblong Square, Lodge dimensions, Masonic ritual, Catechism</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the Masonic catechism for the Fellowcraft degree. Following the description of the Lodge's length and width on the previous page, the dialogue now explores its depth and symbolic location, focusing on the architectural allegories of the Temple of Solomon.</meta>

— 35 —

How deep?
From the surface of the earth to the center.
Where does one find it?
In a secret and hidden place, where peace, rest, unity, and brotherly love reign in the **Valley of J — t** <note>original: "Thal J — t". This refers to the Valley of Jehoshaphat, which in Masonic tradition serves as a symbolic, secluded location for a Lodge to meet "in the valley of the world."</note>.
Upon what is it founded?
Upon three pillars: **Wisdom, Strength,** and **Beauty**.
What do these mean?
**Strength** for the undertaking, **Wisdom** for the execution, and **Beauty** for the decoration.
What ceiling does your **Lodge** <note>The symbol "$\square$" stands for the Lodge.</note> have?
An azure sky, sown with stars without number.
How many windows does it have?
Three; one in the **Morning**, one in the **Evening**, and one at **Midday** <note>These correspond to the East, West, and South.</note>.
Why is there none in the **Midnight** <note>The North.</note>?
Because this region of the world is only dimly lit.
By what is the hall of the Temple decorated?
By two great pillars.
Of what material?
Of **bronze** <note>original: "Erz". In German biblical translations, this refers to the bronze or brass used for the pillars of Solomon's Temple.</note>.
How high are they?
Eighteen **cubits** <term>Cubit (Ellen): An ancient unit of length based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger, used here to match the dimensions of the pillars Jachin and Boaz described in 1 Kings 7.</term>.
Of what circumference?
Twelve **cubits**.

— 36 —

How thick?
Four inches <note>Referring to the thickness of the hollow bronze casting of the pillars.</note>.
What kind of decorations do they have?
Capitals and pedestals.
What do the pillars support?
Two globes, which shine like the sun and the moon.
What is the purpose of these pillars?
The workers' tools and the treasury are kept within them <note>In Masonic tradition, the pillars were hollow to serve as archives for constitutional scrolls and as places to store the "wages" or "treasure" of the craft.</note>.
How does one reach these pillars?
The Apprentices by three steps, the Fellowcrafts by five steps <note>This refers to the "Winding Staircase" of the Temple, where the number of steps symbolizes stages of progress and education in the craft.</note>.
How are the workers admitted there, and in what manner do they receive their wages?
By speaking the password.
What is the password?
S — t <note>original: "S — t". This refers to the password "Shibboleth," which in the Fellowcraft degree signifies "plenty" and is often represented by an ear of corn near a fall of water.</note>.
Does your **Lodge** <note>The symbol "$\square$" represents the Lodge.</note> have no internal decorations?
It has three: the Mosaic pavement, the indented tassel, and the flaming star.
What do these signify?
The Mosaic pavement points to the threshold of the great hall, the indented tassel <term>Indented Tassel (gezackter Quast): Also known as the tessellated border, this represents the skirt of decorative work surrounding the Lodge floor, symbolizing the bond of the brotherhood.</term> to the internal ornaments of the Temple, and the flaming star <term>Flaming Star (flammender Stern): Known as the Blazing Star, it represents the sun, providence, or the light of reason and spiritual guidance.</term> to the center, from which all enlightenment comes.
What is the moral meaning of these images?
Those joined together by plaster and cement <note>The text breaks off here; the "plaster and cement" likely refers to the bond of brotherly love that unites the individual stones (members) into a single structure.</note>

<meta>This page continues the catechism of the Fellowcraft degree. This page elaborates on their symbolic meanings and introduces the "Jewels" of the Lodge.</meta>

— 37 —

Pieces of the mosaic pavement remind us of the strict unity that should prevail among us; the indented tassel <note>Original: "gezackter Quast." This refers to the decorative border of the Lodge floor, symbolizing the bond of the brotherhood.</note> to the ornament that true **Masons** <term>M∴ (Masons): Members of the fraternity.</term> and good **Brothers** <term>B. B∴ (Brothers): From the German 'Brüder', referring to the fraternal membership.</term> provide to a **Lodge** <note>The symbol ▭ represents the Lodge.</note>; and the flaming star to the great **Grand Architect of the Universe** <term>B. M∴ der W∴ (Baumeister der Welt): Literally "Master Builder of the World," a title for the Creator used in Masonic ritual.</term>, from whose gaze none of our actions escape.

How many jewels do you have?

Six: three movable and three immovable; the movable ones are the **Square** <term>Winkelmaas (Square): A tool used by stonemasons to ensure corners are perfectly 90 degrees; in a moral sense, it represents acting "on the square" or with integrity.</term>, the **Level** <term>Niveau (Level): A tool used to find a horizontal plane; it symbolizes equality among members.</term>, and the **Plumb Rule** <term>Senkblei (Plumb Rule): A tool used to ensure vertical uprightness; it symbolizes rectitude and justice.</term>.

What is the purpose of these?

With the help of the Square, one draws correct right angles; the Level helps us draw lines parallel to the **East** <term>Orient (East): In Masonic symbolism, the East represents the source of light and the location of the Master's chair.</term>; and the Plumb Rule serves for the erection of upright buildings.

Is there not also a moral meaning in these images?

Yes: the Square should teach us that all our actions must be guided by justice and fairness; the Level signifies that a perfect equality should prevail among the **Brothers**; and the Plumb Rule alludes to the integrity in our judgments, which must align only with the truth and never allow itself to be bent by schemes, by cabals <note>In this context, "cabals" refers to secret political factions or intrigues.</note>, or by personal and particular inclinations.

Which are the three immovable jewels?

The **Rough Ashlar** <term>rohe Stein (Rough Ashlar): An unhewn stone as it comes from the quarry, representing the initiate's unrefined state.</term>, the **Tracing Board** <term>Reißbrett (Tracing Board): A board on which the Master draws his plans; it symbolizes the divine plan or the moral blueprint for a life.</term>, and the **Cubic Stone** <term>kubische Stein (Cubic Stone or Perfect Ashlar): A stone smoothed and squared by the worker's labor, representing the refined character of an experienced Mason.</term>; the Rough Ashlar serves the Apprentices...

— 38 —

...to teach the work; on the **cubic stone** <term>kubischer Stein (Perfect Ashlar): A stone shaped into a perfect cube, representing the initiated man who has refined his character through education and virtue.</term>, the **Fellows** <term>Gesellen (Fellowcrafts): The second rank of Freemasonry, corresponding to journeymen in medieval guilds.</term> sharpen the tools of the Masters, and on the **tracing board** <term>Reißbrett (Tracing Board): A board or floor cloth where the Master draws the architectural plans for the building.</term> the Masters draft their drawings?

What is their moral significance?

The **rough stone** <term>roher Stein (Rough Ashlar): An unhewn stone fresh from the quarry, symbolizing man in his natural, uneducated state.</term> shows that our soul is capable of both good and evil impressions; the **cubic stone** teaches us to watch over our actions incessantly, so that we may keep ourselves safe from wrongdoing; the **tracing board** is a symbol of the good examples that should ease our practice of virtue, and by which we should guide one another.

Why do the three officers of the **Lodge** <note>The symbol □ represents the Lodge.</note> use wooden gavels?

Because during the construction of **Solomon's Temple** <note>The central allegorical building in Masonic tradition, based on the biblical Temple in Jerusalem.</note>, no noise from metal tools was to be heard within its interior; stone and wood were already prepared in the quarry and the forest, and thus it was only a matter of putting them in place.

What is the purpose of the images of the Sun and the Moon in their **Lodge** <note>The symbol □ represents the Lodge.</note>?

The Sun illuminates, and the Moon guides our labors.

Is there not also a moral meaning in these images?

They teach us that we should never grow weary in the practice of virtue, that the highest...

...Being sees us always, and that every day which the sun brings to us also brings us closer to eternity.

What does the **globe** signify in your **Lodge** <note>The symbol □ represents the Lodge.</note>?
That **Freemasonry** <note>The abbreviation F$\therefore$ My$\therefore$ stands for Freimaurerei (Freemasonry).</note> is spread across the entire surface of the earth.

What is the purpose of the **trowel**?
To cover the faults of the brothers. <note>This refers to the "cement" of brotherly love which hides imperfections in others.</note>

How long do you work for your Master?
From Monday noon until Saturday midnight.

Which qualities must a good **Mason** <note>The abbreviation M$\therefore$ stands for Maurer (Mason).</note> possess?
Discretion, zeal, quiet wisdom, and brotherly love.

Where are the **Fellows** <term>Gesellen (Fellowcrafts): The second rank of Freemasonry, corresponding to journeymen in medieval guilds.</term> in the **Lodge**?
On the midnight side <note>The North.</note> towards the morning <note>The East.</note>, or in the midday <note>The South.</note> behind the Masters whom they are to serve.

Where are they paid?
At **Pillar B.** <term>Säule B. (Pillar Boaz): One of the two bronze pillars at the entrance of Solomon’s Temple; in this rite, it is where the Fellowcrafts receive their wages.</term>

How old are you as a Fellow?
Five years. <note>This is a symbolic age representing the time of study and the five senses, rather than actual years lived.</note>

Why?

They follow the example of the sun, which ends its course in the evening; they end the work, and pay the workers.

At midnight.

All enter with the usual gait and give the **password**.

# Questions upon Entry into the Lodge <note>The symbol $\Box$ is a standard Masonic shorthand for a Lodge.</note>

Where do you come from, my Brothers? <note>original: B. B.</note>
From the **St. John’s Lodge**, <term>Johannes-Loge: In Freemasonry, "St. John's Lodges" refer to the basic units of the craft that work the first three degrees (Apprentice, Fellowcraft, and Master Mason).</term>
What do you bring?
Peace, health, and a good welcome to the **Worshipful Master** <note>original: E. M. (Ehrwürdiger Meister)</note> and all Brothers.
What do you desire here?
To learn to overcome our passions, and to subject our will to the laws of **Freemasonry**. <note>original: F:. My:. (Freimaurerei)</note>
What duties does a **Mason** <note>original: M:.</note> have in the Lodge?
To work united with his Brothers for the progress of the **Royal Art**, <term>Königliche Kunst: A traditional term for Freemasonry, suggesting it is a noble pursuit of self-improvement and wisdom.</term> in order to become worthy of attaining higher degrees and disclosures.
Help me, then, my Brothers, to achieve this purpose, and proceed to your places.

**Brother Apprentices!** <term>Lehrlinge: The first rank or degree of Freemasonry.</term> Because of the good testimonials regarding his conduct, and because his Masters are satisfied with him, we have just initiated Brother N. N. <note>Placeholder for the name of the specific member.</note> as a **Fellowcraft**, <term>Geselle: The second degree of Masonry.</term> in order to reward his zeal and his labors; let this example spur your emulation; from now on, you shall recognize him as a Fellowcraft, and by virtue of this degree, grant him precedence and follow his instructions.

The Brother Apprentices signal their approval by raising their hands.

Brothers, Brothers, does any Brother have anything to propose for the good of the Lodge?

Why?

Following the example of the sun, which [and so forth]. <note>The text abbreviates a standard ritual response: just as the sun sets in the west to close the day, the Warden sits in the west to close the Lodge.</note>

How long do the Apprentices work?

From Monday noon until Saturday midnight.

At what time do they leave their work?

At midnight.

**Brother 2nd Warden**: what is the hour? <note>original: was ist die Glocke? (literally "what is the bell/clock?")</note>

**High Midnight**. <term>High Midnight: The symbolic time at which Masonic labor is traditionally said to end.</term>

The **Worshipful Master**: [knocks twice, then once] — [oo — o].

The **1st Warden**: [oo — o], The **2nd Warden**: [oo — o].

The **sign** and the **battery**. <term>Battery: A rhythmic sequence of claps or knocks (Beifallsbezeugung) used to show approval or to close a ritual segment.</term>

## Initiation
---

My Brothers, in the event that any of you should find cause for the exclusion of the candidate, let us proceed once more to the collection of votes.

The Masters recovered themselves when they saw the body of Hiram with his hand on his heart and his foot at a right angle. One of them took his hand in the manner of the **Apprentices** <term>Apprentices<note>Original: "Lehrlinge." Referring to the first degree of Freemasonry, the Entered Apprentice.</note></term> and tried to lift him thus, but he kept only the index finger in his hand <note>In the ritual narrative, the body has decayed so much that the skin or flesh slips off when the initial grips are attempted.</note>; another Master tried to lift him by the finger **B.** <note>Referring to the "Fellowcraft" grip associated with the pillar Boaz.</note>, but he met the same fate as the former. The Masters, seeing this effect of decay, cried out with one voice **M.. B..**, which in the holy language <note>Referring to Hebrew, though the phrase is a Masonic corruption of Hebrew roots.</note> means as much as: *the flesh is loosening from the bones*: thereupon a third grasped Hiram's entire hand with a full grip, placed an arm under his back, set his knee against the knee of the deceased, and lifted him up in this way, in order to bring him to Jerusalem, where **Solomon** had a golden triangular plate placed on his tomb with the inscription: **Jehovah**, which in the Hebrew language is the name of the **Eternal**, and in ancient times was the **Master's Word**.

Now approach the foot of the Altar to take the Master's Oath.

- 61 -

and by the circumstances of my reception, as well as by the death of our Master Hiram. <note>Hiram Abiff is the central allegorical figure of the Master Mason degree, a master architect of Solomon's Temple who, according to Masonic legend, was murdered by three ruffians.</note>
**Where were you received as a Master?**
In a dark place, where there was weeping and wailing in the inner chamber.
**In what manner did you arrive there?**
By a winding staircase, which one ascends by 3, 5, and 7. <note>These numbers refer to the steps of the winding staircase in the Temple, representing the three who rule a Lodge, the five who compose it, and the seven who make it perfect.</note>
**What do these numbers signify?**
3 constitute the essence of a <term>▢</term> <note>The symbol ▢ stands for the Lodge in Masonic texts.</note>, 5 compose it, and 7 make it perfect.
**What opened the inner chamber to you?**
Nine strong blows.
**What did you notice upon entering the ▢?**
A faint glimmer of light, deep silence, and bitter grief.
**What was the cause of this?**
The death of our Worshipful Master <note>Original: "E. M." likely for *Ehrwürdiger Meister*.</note> Hiram.
**Who was Hiram?**
A skillful artisan from Tyre; his father was named Ur, and his mother was a widow from the tribe of Naphtali. <note>This identifies the candidate with the "Son of the Widow," a common epithet for Masons.</note>
**How were you received?**
By passing from the square to the compass, over the grave of our Worshipful Master Hiram. <note>The Square and Compass are the primary tools of Masonry; the Master's degree focuses on the Compass, symbolizing spiritual boundaries.</note>
**Were you a Fellow Craft before you became a Master?**
J. and B. were known to me. <note>J. and B. stand for Jachin and Boaz, the two pillars of Solomon’s Temple, representing the first two degrees of Masonry.</note>
**What does the Master’s Word mean?**
The flesh is loosening from the bones. <note>This refers to the state of Hiram's body when it was discovered in the legend, symbolizing the transition from death to spiritual rebirth.</note>

- 62 -

**What does the password mean?**
->Excellence.<- <note>Original: *Vortreflichkeit*. In some Masonic traditions, this refers to the Hebrew word "Shibboleth" or "Tubal-cain," though here it is being interpreted by its moral quality.</note>
**What do the nine lights in the <term>▢</term> signify?** <note>The symbol ▢ represents the Lodge.</note>
->The nine Master Masons sent out to search for Hiram.<- <note>Original: *H.:* for Hiram, *M.:* for *Meister* (Masters). This refers to the nine brothers appointed by King Solomon to find the missing architect.</note>
**What did the Master Masons do after the death of Hiram?**
They clothed themselves in white gloves, as proof that they had never stained their hands with the blood of their superiors or their brothers. <note>White gloves in Masonry traditionally symbolize innocence and the purity of the heart.</note>
**Upon what does their <term>▢</term> rest?**
Upon three triangular columns: Wisdom, Strength, and Beauty. Wisdom in undertaking, Strength for execution, and Beauty for ornamentation. <note>These three pillars are central to Masonic architecture; Wisdom is associated with Solomon, Strength with King Hiram of Tyre, and Beauty with the master builder Hiram Abiff.</note>
**What are the five perfect points of the Master Masons?**
The foot-to-foot, the kneeling, the joining of hands, the left arm on the shoulder, and the kiss of peace. <note>These are known as the "Five Points of Fellowship," physical positions used during the ritual to demonstrate the bond between brothers.</note>
**Will you give me a closer explanation of this?**
The sign with the foot signifies that we should always be ready to hasten to the aid of our brothers; the kneeling, that we should always humble ourselves before Him who gave us our existence; the joining of hands, that we should support our fellow brothers; the left arm on the shoulder, that we should help our fellow brothers <note>Original: *B.: B.:* for *Beliebte Brüder* (Beloved Brothers).</note> with philanthropic and gentle counsel, and that, should they fall, our duties [require us] to lift them up— <note>The sentence ends abruptly, continuing onto the next page.</note>

...direct [our prayers to heaven]; <note>The text here completes the thought from the previous page regarding the "kneeling" position. **Upon what do the Masters work?**
Upon the drawing board.

**With what do you work?**
With chalk, charcoal, and cement. <note>In Masonic symbolism, these represent freedom, fervency, and zeal.</note>

**What moral sense lies in this <term>hieroglyph</term>?** <note>Hieroglyph: Here referring to a symbolic picture or emblem used for moral instruction.</note>
That purity, zeal, and unity must animate the Masters.

**How do the Masters travel?**
From the East toward the West, in order to receive the light once again.

**If they have lost one of their Brothers, where will they seek him?**
Between the square and the compass.

**Why?**
The square and compass are symbols of wisdom and justice, from which a true Master must never distance himself.

**What is a Master called?** Gabaon. <note>Original: *Gabaon*. A biblical reference to Gibeon, often used in Masonic ritual to signify a place of great significance or the name of a Master.</note>

**His son?** Lufton. <note>Original: *Lufton*. This is a Germanized version of the French *louveteau* (wolf-cub). In English Freemasonry, the son of a Master Mason is called a "Lewis."</note>

**What are the privileges of a Lufton?**
To be chosen as a Master before kings and princes.

**What would you do if you were in danger?**
I would make the sign of assistance and cry out: "To me, children of the widow!" <note>The phrase "Children of the Widow" refers to the Brotherhood. According to legend, Hiram Abiff, the builder of Solomon's temple, was the son of a widow of the tribe of Naphtali.</note>

— 64 —

Why do they say **Children of the Widow**?
The Masters had always regarded **H[iram]** <note>Hiram Abiff, the master architect of Solomon's Temple and central figure of Masonic ritual.</note> as their **B[rother]**, and after his death, they took care of his mother; because of this, they called themselves her children. <note>This explains the traditional Masonic self-designation as "Sons of the Widow."</note>

How old are you as a Master?
Seven years and more.

Why?
Because Solomon required that much time for the construction of the Temple.

Until midnight.

Full midnight.

What do you seek here?
The light that was lost.

Through three strong blows, etc. <note>A reference to the legendary murder of Hiram Abiff by three ruffians seeking the secrets of a Master.</note>

Why do they say **Children of the Widow**?
The Masters had always regarded **H[iram]** <note>Hiram Abiff, the master architect of Solomon’s Temple and central figure of Masonic ritual.</note> as their **B[rother]**, and after his death, they took care of his mother; because of this, they called themselves her children. <note>This explains the traditional Masonic self-designation as "Sons of the Widow," symbolizing the bonds of brotherhood that extend to caring for a deceased member's family.</note>

Seven years and more.

Why?
Because Solomon required that much time for the construction of the Temple. <note>In Masonic symbolism, the age of a member corresponds to the time spent on stages of the Temple's construction, rather than chronological years.</note>

# Junior Scotch Mason

First, a Master’s Lodge is opened; the Master Mason who is to be admitted as a Junior Scotch Mason is placed between the two Wardens <note>In Masonic lodges, the Wardens are the senior officers who assist the Master in governing the lodge.</note> and strictly examined as a Junior Master; afterward, he is led back to the preparation room, and the Master’s Lodge is then closed with all the usual ceremonies.

## Opening of the Lodge

The Master strikes a blow upon the drawing board <term>drawing board: also known as a tracing board, this is a wooden board or canvas used in Freemasonry to display the symbols of a specific degree</term> and speaks: I request you, Exalted Brethren, to assist me in the opening and conduct of a Scotch St. Andrew's Lodge. <note>The symbol $\boxtimes$ represents "Lodge" in this context.</note>

**Question:** Exalted Brother First Warden! Is it the proper time to open a Scotch Initiation Lodge?

The First Warden draws his sword, greets the Master with a bow, and speaks:

**Answer:** Right Worshipful and Exalted Knight, it is the proper time to open a Scotch Lodge.

**Command:** Let the Brother Second [Warden]...

The Master speaks: Bring this newly admitted Scottish <term>Brother: *B.:*</term> back toward the West <note>original: *nach Abend*; literally "towards evening," the traditional Masonic direction representing the West</note>, and let him see the radiance of the <term>blazing star: *flammenden Sterns*</term>.

The other <term>Brothers: *B. B.:*</term> make the Young Scottish sign with the Master, and once the eyes of the newly admitted have been opened by the <term>Senior Warden: *ersten Aufseher*</term>, the Master speaks: Behold, my sublime <term>Brother: *B.:*</term>, the radiance of the <term>blazing star: *flammenden Sterns*</term>.

> (NB. In true Scottish Lodges, the <term>blazing star: *flammende Stern*</term> stands illuminated with 81 lamps above the head of the Master.)

Then the new <term>Brother: *B.:*</term> is led to make the four Scottish steps, and is shown how he should thereby approach the <term>throne: *Stuhle*</term> <note>the Master's ceremonial chair</note>. He is then given the sign, grip, and <term>word: *Wort*</term>: *Adonay* <note>original Hebrew: "Lord," used here as a sacred password</note>. A green ribbon cockade is placed on his hat, the Scottish apron is tied on him, and the significance of the green color—namely, hope—is explained to him...

This now signifies the half-open door and the sun shining through it.

Now we wish to present the carpet <note>In Freemasonry, the "carpet" or tracing board is a visual aid illustrating the symbols and allegories of a specific degree.</note> in three longitudinal sections and briefly go through them together.

If we begin on the right-hand side, we first find the **Tower of Babel**; it is known that this was a foolish undertaking, highly displeasing to the Creator, by the descendants of Cain, who sought to ensure their safety against any future flood <note>original: *Strafwasser* — literally "punishment-water," referring to the Great Deluge.</note>. They also wished to make themselves great through external, sensual pleasures and an unforgettable name; yet through this, they believed they were achieving the true purpose of the **Order**. The same happens even in the present time; the majority of our nominal **Brothers** <note>original: *B. B..*, likely standing for *Brüder* (Brothers).</note> misunderstand the right purpose; they consider it a melancholic invention and attach all sorts of vulgar nicknames to its teachings. Instead, they either build a tower of all kinds of sensual pleasures or dream of conquests and hatch financial plans for them. Obedience and willingness are coerced so that the self-imposed so-called "High Superiors" can, undisturbed and lustfully, consume the tribute extorted from the lulled, weak-believing lower members. Genuine, working **Freemasons** are exposed to persecutions; **Wisdom** is mocked, and **Truth** is turned away from their doors.

...which is symbolized by these **three golden bowls**.

In the first stood a Hebrew **Yod** <note>original: *Jod* — the tenth letter of the Hebrew alphabet, often representing a divine point of light or the starting point of creation.</note>, by which the **pure spiritual salt-body** is to be understood. In the second stood a **Gimel** <note>original: *Gimel* — the third letter of the Hebrew alphabet.</note> or **G.**, which indicates the **fire of life**. The **volatile spirit**, however, cannot be expressed in a single name; therefore, this bowl is not marked with any letter. **Solomon** presented these three bowls to his **Master Builder** at the construction of the Temple, who had hidden them in a hollowed-out stone; however, four elder masters brought them from beneath the rubble of the Temple and fled to **Scotland** during the Order's final, sorrowful epoch. There, they gradually spread the persecuted teachings of the Order once more and kept the **jewel** contained within the three bowls in safe custody. They are still kept in the **Scottish Lodge at Edinburgh**; for this reason, all true masters experienced in **Scottish Masonry** are called **Master Builders** <note>original: *Werkmeister* — literally "work-master," a title implying both architectural skill and spiritual mastery.</note>.

### Interpretation

By this they wish to say briefly how it is possible for a true **master-craftsman** <term>Werkmeister: A term used here to bridge the gap between a Masonic "Master Mason" and an alchemical "Master of the Work" (Artifex).</term> to unite the solidified water, then the air with both, and finally the fire with those three already combined. **Question:** What did you obtain from this?

**Answer:** A body without frailty <note>This refers to the "Incorruptible Body" or the "Philosopher's Stone," which was believed to grant health and longevity.</note>.

### Interpretation

They thereby indicate the precise and inseparable union of the four properties, which after their maturation becomes a **universal remedy** <note>Often called the "Panacea," a substance capable of curing all diseases and "perfecting" base metals into gold.</note> in the general sense.

<meta>This page transitions from the general alchemical interpretation of the elements into the formal ritual catechism of the "Young Scottish" degree. ...the **Young Scots** have drawn me out of the **Master Mason** <note>original: "MMstr:" (Meister Maurer). The text refers to the transition from the standard Blue Lodge degrees to the higher "Scottish" degrees.</note> assembly.

**Question:** Where were you received?
**Answer:** At the grave of the Master, amidst the **Cassia** <note>In Masonic tradition, the sprig of Acacia (often called Cassia in older texts) symbolizes immortality and marks the grave of the master architect, Hiram Abiff.</note>.

**Question:** How were you received?
**Answer:** I was first led as an **Apprentice** <note>The first degree of Masonry.</note> through midnight, then as a **Fellow Craft** <note>The second degree of Masonry.</note> through noon and from the morning, and finally through noon, morning, and midnight back into evening.

**Question:** Why were you led in this way?
**Answer:** To obtain wisdom, art, virtue, and the pleasure of the Creator.

**Question:** Where were you led as a **Young Scot**?
**Answer:** Between the Sun and the Moon into the **Holy of Holies** <note>The innermost sanctum of the Temple of Solomon.</note>, because I was to strip the leaves from the ripe Cassia branch—that is, I was to learn to divide the **glorious matter** of our Work <term>Matter of our Work: The "Prima Materia" or the raw starting material used in alchemy to create the Philosopher's Stone.</term>.

**Question:** What did you learn to recognize under the Sun?
**Answer:** Just as the Sun draws everything from nature to itself by its power to glorify it with light-particles, and makes it full of electric-fiery properties, so too did I learn to prepare the **Fire of the Sages** <term>Fire of the Sages: A "secret fire" or non-combustive heat used by alchemists to mature their substances without burning them.</term>, which is a seed of gold and equal to solar fire in its indestructibility.

**Question:** What did you learn to recognize under the Moon?

**Answer:** Just as the **Moon** receives the ripened power of light, causes it to coagulate, and flows it down into the water—the first **womb**—so too did I have to make the dissolved **Body of the Order** <note>In this context, the "Body of the Order" likely refers to the symbolic matter being worked on in the alchemical vessel, which represents both the candidate and the substance of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> coagulate, grow, and become living again in thirteen days.

**Question:** Do you have further instruction in the secrets of our sublime Order?

**Answer:** Yes! I learned to unite the forces of nature, namely: the warm with the cold, the cold with the moist, and the moist with the dry.

**Question:** What did you obtain from this?

**Answer:** A body without frailty, in which an ever-active life exists in an inseparable union.

**Question:** For what purpose does this work serve you?

**Answer:** For the preservation of health and for the refinement <term>Refinement: Or "ennobling" (Veredlung), the process of purifying "base" metals like lead into "noble" metals like silver or gold.</term> of the six immature **metals**. <note>The "six metals" typically refer to lead, tin, iron, copper, mercury, and silver, which the alchemist seeks to "ripen" into gold.</note>

**Question:** What then have you learned since entering the Order?

**Answer:** To know the **primal matter** of all beings, to prepare the **Chaos** <note>original: "Chaos." In alchemy, this refers to the "massa confusa," the raw, disorganized starting material before it is purified and structured.</note>, to separate the true beginnings, to unite them naturally, and to bring them to ripeness.

**Question:** Where were you rewarded?

**Answer:** In the Holy Place, through the knowledge of the first beginning, the two genders <note>Likely referring to the "Chemical Wedding" of the masculine (Sulfur) and feminine (Mercury) principles.</note>, their three components, four forces, and seven properties.

**Question:** Are you then a free **Young Scot**?

**Answer:** Yes! I was clothed in white and green—

...worked, adorned with the **sign of freedom**, because I have been instructed how the **flaming star** <note>The "flaming star" (original: *flammende Stern*), or Blazing Star, is a central symbol in Masonic lodges, representing Divine Providence, the Sun, or the alchemical "quintessence" that guides the seeker.</note> is to be prepared.

**Question:** What time is it?
**Answer:** Past midnight. <note>In ritual work, midnight represents the symbolic end of the labor and the transition into the deepest mysteries, where the light of the new day (or new spiritual insight) begins to emerge from the darkness.</note>

# Fifth Degree.

->Of<-

->the true<-

->working Masonry<- <note>The term "working Masonry" (original: *arbeitenden Maurerei*) distinguishes the active, spiritual practice and ritual labor from purely social or theoretical "speculative" Freemasonry.</note>

->or<-

->Scottish Old-Master<- <note>The "Scottish Old-Master" (original: *Schottischen Alt-Meister*) is a high-ranking degree in 18th-century systems. Despite the name, these degrees often originated in France and Germany rather than Scotland, claiming to possess "older" or "higher" secrets than the standard three degrees of the lodge.</note>

->and<-

->Knight of Saint Andrew.<- <note>Saint Andrew is the patron saint of Scotland. In this context, the "Knight of Saint Andrew" refers to a specific chivalric grade of Masonry that emphasizes themes of loyalty, defense of the order, and Christian mysticism.</note>

### Opening of the Chapter of Reception.

The Grand Master and Knight of the Chair strikes the table once with the gavel and says:

> “Prepare yourselves, my sublime brethren, for the opening of a Scottish Knights' Chapter.”

After the ensuing silence, and when all Knights <term>Knights</term><note>original: "RRr.," an abbreviation for "Ritter."</note> have made the Chapter Sign <note>A specific ritual gesture or hand signal used to identify members of this specific degree.</note>, the presiding officer continues:

— III —

[The Brother] rises from his chair, keeps his hat on his head, draws his sword, makes his salute with it toward the Chair <term>the Chair</term><note>The "Chair" refers to the "Master of the Chair," the presiding officer of the lodge or chapter.</note>, and gives the following answer:

> Answer: Highly Worshipful and Sublime Knight of the Chair! It is according to the regulations of our ancestors to see: whether the doors are locked, how this assembled Chapter may maintain itself, and whether all secret and public ambushes are removed, so that we may be and remain covered and secure.

The Master says hereupon: Let it be seen whether our doors are guarded by Knights.

The First Overseer <term>First Overseer</term><note>original: "Aufseher." In Masonic contexts, this usually refers to the Senior Warden.</note> conveys this command to the Second Senior Overseer; the latter stands up, draws his sword, salutes the Chair, goes, and sees whether the doors are locked and guarded on the inside by the youngest Knight. Once this has been done and the Second Senior Overseer has returned, he brings the report to the First Warden <term>Warden</term><note>original: "Vorsteher," another term used here for the presiding officers.</note>, and he in turn to the Chair.

> Highly Worshipful and Sublime Knight, the doors are locked and the Knights <note>original: "RRr.," an abbreviation for "Ritter" (Knights).</note> are at their watch; our Chapter is secure.

The Master of the Chair asks the Second Senior Overseer, who meanwhile has sat down again and sheathed his sword.

> Question: My Highly Honorable Brother <term>Brother</term><note>original: "B..," an abbreviation for "Bruder."</note>, Second Senior Overseer: how many does the number of the Scottish Knights’ Chapter amount to?

...components, 4 the properties, 5 the light-being <note>original: "Lichtwesen." This likely refers to the spiritual or astral essence within the Masonic/alchemical cosmology.</note>, 7 the forces, 9 the end of all things.

Question: Why is that?

Question: Who loves wisdom?
Answer: We do!

Question: Who will protect her?
Answer: We will!

Question: What must one do to obtain wisdom?
Answer: Cast off **self-love and vice**.

Question: Where are these found?
Answer: Within us.

Question: Are they visible?
Answer: Not immediately; rather, a **Past Master** <term>Past Master</term><note>original: "Alt-Meister." A rank or degree signifying one who has presided over a Lodge.</note> must apply understanding, make use of time, and have the courage to clear away all investigated obstacles.

Question: Which is the most beautiful color?

Answer: The Astral, the Animal, the Vegetable, and the Mineral. <note>These represent the four "kingdoms" or levels of existence in the Hermetic tradition.</note>

Question: Is anything higher known to you?
Answer: Yes! It is known to me and to us all, but it must not be named. <note>A reference to the Ineffable Name of God or the ultimate secret of the Order that transcends verbal expression.</note>

Question: How did you come to know it?
Answer: When I found the **lost word** <term>Lost Word</term><note>original: "verlohrne Wort." A central Masonic myth regarding a secret word (often the true name of God) that was lost and which the initiate must seek to recover.</note>.

Question: Where is it found?
Answer: In the East and the West. <note>original: "Im Morgen und Abend." Literally "In the morning and evening," referring to the rising and setting of the sun and the symbolic orientation of the Lodge.</note>

When the **candidate** has answered this question satisfactorily, the **Grand Master** commands the **Second Senior Warden** to lead the **aspirant** out of the hall—that is: as an **Apprentice** and **Fellow** into the outer court, from there into the **Sanctuary** as a **St. John’s Master** and as a **Young Scottish Master**, and now as a future **Old Master** into the **Holy of Holies**, to the three golden bowls.

Thereupon a fourfold circuit takes place, and as often as the initiate comes before the altar, the **Master of the Chair** gives the four **Young Scottish knocks**, and makes the sign, which the present **Brothers** <term>B.⸫</term> likewise make.

At the first four knocks, the **Grand Master of the Chair** says: "O! how great is the splendor of God's glory between the **Cherubim**!" At the second four knocks, the **Grand Master** says: "We worship the great, most holy **Jehovah** <note>original: "Jehova." This name is often used in high-degree Masonry to represent the most sacred name of the Divine.</note>, who lives from eternity to eternity." At the third four knocks: "Wondrous and worthy of adoration is the radiant light upon the **Ark of the Covenant** <note>original: "Bundes-Lade."</note>."

At the fourth four knocks: "Let us with...

## Instruction

Sublime and venerable **Knight** and **Brother**! Through the step you have now taken, you have been brought before the curtain, and are rightly numbered among the **Scottish Old Masters**, upon whom the reflection of the **Eastern Light** <note>A common Masonic metaphor for divine wisdom or the source of enlightenment, often associated with the Orient.</note> already shines. You shall—in and through the connection of your previous works—bring the essence of all things, freed from earthiness, to the point that it bears more than a thousandfold fruit; that is: that one part of the strengthened **primal matter** <term>Urstoff</term> <note>In alchemy, this refers to the *Prima Materia*, the base substance required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note> ripens a thousand or more parts of unripened metals. Through your previous work, you gained insight into how the **number 4** is to be explained, and what mystical sense was hidden within it. Now, however, the possibility shall be shown to you of what mystical sense is hidden within the **numbers from 1 to 10**. All of this will be made quite clear and comprehensible to you through the lectures in the **Chapter**. Now I will give you a brief explanation of the ceremonies performed during the admission into the sublime **Chapter of Old Masters**.

Before your admission, you remained with the watching **Brethren** <note>original: "B. B.∴", an abbreviation for *Brüder* (Brethren) followed by the Masonic triangular dots.</note> in the choir; you waited for your service in the holy work of our glorious **Square** <note>In this tradition, the square represents the four elements and the "four-fold" nature of the physical world that must be brought into harmony.</note>, which created the four pure properties of our **Stone of Filling** <term>Stein der Füllung</term> <note>Likely referring to the "Stone of Plenitude" or a stage of the Philosopher’s Stone where it is "filled" with its power.</note> that are active in its pure essence. From this, one and two are to be separated from three—namely, two spiritual and invisible properties as an active fire separated from the suffering spiritual water. Through the connection of these two, a shining body is formed. Then, by the joining of these first two components—the **spiritual solar sulfur** with the **lunar sulfur**—and their exact union through a pure **mercurial salt**, an imperishable being is sired. If you have thus shrouded an airy **mercurial soul** within a pure salt-body, and ignited within it the growing life-force through the fiery spirit, then you have already brought your work to the **rest-number 10** <note>The number 10, or the Decad, symbolizes completion and the return to unity after the labor of the lower numbers.</note>, which is what your lying upon the **red carpet** in the form of the **high cross** signifies.

### Closing Dialogue

**Q.** Are you then a **Scottish Old Master** and **Knight**?
**A.** My Superior Master and fellow Old Masters recognize me as such.
**Q.** By what sign?
**A.** I am fully acquainted with the **number 4** <note>In this tradition, the number 4 often represents the Tetragrammaton (the four-letter name of God) or the four elements brought into perfect balance.</note>.
**Q.** Where were you received?
**A.** In the **Scottish Isles** <note>In the lore of the "Scottish" degrees, the Scottish Isles (often referring to Kilwinning or the Hebrides) were presented as the mythical refuge of the Knights Templar and the cradle of true Masonry.</note>.
**Q.** What did you see at your reception?
**A.** Three golden bowls; in the first was a **J**, in the second a **G**, in the third...

**Answer.** Because its **fire-power** is still simple <note>In this context, "simple" means uncompounded or in its raw, singular state, lacking the necessary intensity for the Great Work.</note>, it must first be doubled by **gold**, which is full of **light-power**.

<meta>This page continues the alchemical catechism from page 143, detailing the process of increasing the potency of the "Stone" or the spiritualized gold.
